340 — Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP)

Project Number: 340

Project Name: Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP)

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

Goal: Implement, from scratch in Rust, a Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP) engine that instruments a running application from the inside to detect and block attacks in real time with full request + execution context: a hook/instrumentation ABI intercepting dangerous sinks (SQL exec, command exec, file open, outbound HTTP, deserialization, LDAP/XXE), a per-request execution-context store with thread/async-local propagation, a taint-tracking core (HTTP sources -> propagation -> sink checks), a per-sink detection engine (SQLi, command injection, path traversal, SSRF, deserialization, XXE/LDAP), a monitor/block policy + enforcement layer, and low-overhead inline protection with SIEM telemetry (crates rasp-core/hook/context/taint/detect/policy/protect/telemetry). It runs inside the app runtime via a hook ABI — unlike a network-edge WAF (project 338) — and must never crash the host (fail-open/fail-safe). Validated by an OWASP attack corpus, a benign false-positive corpus, analyzer fuzzing, and an overhead budget (<5% p95).

1. Goal

1.1 Engineering Definition

Build a Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP) engine that instruments a running application from the inside to detect and block injection attacks at the moment dangerous operations execute. The guard runs inline in every request, intercepting sink operations (SQL exec, OS command exec, file open, outbound HTTP, deserialization, LDAP query, XML parse) via a hook ABI, correlates intercepted arguments against per-request taint derived from HTTP input, and either allows or blocks the operation with full evidence attachment.

1.2 System Boundary

Inside boundary Outside boundary
Hook ABI + dispatch (rasp-hook) Bytecode weaving / JVM agent
Per-request context store (rasp-context) APM / distributed tracing backend
Taint source ingestion + propagation (rasp-taint) Network-edge WAF (project 338)
Per-sink analyzers: SQLi, cmdi, pathtraversal, SSRF, deser, XXE, LDAP/NoSQL (rasp-detect) SAST / DAST scanners
Policy + enforcement + virtual patching (rasp-policy + rasp-protect) EDR / host-kernel agent
Telemetry export: SIEM/CEF, metrics, audit (rasp-telemetry) Language-specific runtime adapters (out-of-core)
Config + CLI driver (rasp-cli) ML/AI threat scoring
Benchmark + attack corpus (rasp-bench) Container orchestration / service mesh

1.3 Explicit Non-Goals

  • No network packet capture; no HTTP traffic interception at OS layer.
  • No SAST source analysis; no DAST external scanner.
  • No EDR host-kernel protection (syscall hooks, eBPF, kprobes).
  • No per-runtime bytecode weaving implementation (Java agent, .NET Profiler, Node.js --require); adapters are specified but out-of-core.
  • No ML/AI behavioral anomaly models in Phase 0–5.
  • No GUI dashboard; telemetry is export-only (CEF/JSON).
  • No automatic vulnerability remediation; blocks only, no code patching.
  • No cross-process taint tracking.
  • No cryptographic key management or secret scanning.

1.4 Deployment Model

  • Embedded library: linked into the protected process; communicates via hook ABI function calls, not sockets.
  • Adapter layer: per-runtime shim (JNI, FFI, Node N-API, WASM host) calls the Rust ABI at sink entry/exit.
  • Policy file: TOML/JSON loaded at startup; hot-reloadable via SIGHUP or file watcher.
  • Telemetry: out-of-process SIEM/CEF receiver or local log file; non-blocking async channel.
  • Fail mode: configurable fail-open (allow on RASP error) or fail-closed (block on RASP error); default fail-open for brownfield, fail-closed for greenfield.

1.5 Target Users/Operators/Developers

Persona Use case
AppSec engineer Deploy RASP in Monitor mode, tune rules, promote to Block
Platform/SRE Integrate telemetry into SIEM, monitor overhead metrics
Developer Annotate taint sources in custom frameworks, write virtual patches
Penetration tester Validate detection coverage with attack corpus (rasp-bench)
Security researcher Extend rasp-detect with new sink analyzers

1.6 Phase-0 Deliverable — Crate Inventory

Numbered crates constituting the Phase-0 lockstep deliverable:

  1. rasp-core — canonical types: SinkType, HookEvent, CallContext, RequestContext, TaintSource, TaintTag, TaintedValue, Detection, Action, Rule, Policy, Severity, RaspError.
  2. rasp-hook — hook ABI (sink_entry, sink_exit), registration, runtime-adapter interface, safe trampolines, multi-sink dispatch.
  3. rasp-context — per-request store (thread-local + async task-local), HTTP request capture, call stack, correlation id, lifecycle (begin/end).
  4. rasp-taint — taint sources, TaintTag creation, propagation policy (string concat/slice/format), sink-time checks, bounded-cost tracking.
  5. rasp-detect — per-sink analyzers: SQLi (libinjection-style tokeniser), command injection, path traversal, SSRF, deserialization gadget check, XXE, LDAP/NoSQL injection; signature + semantic rules; Detection output.
  6. rasp-policy — Policy evaluation: monitor vs block, per-rule Action, virtual patching, waivers, fail-open/fail-closed.
  7. rasp-protect — inline guard: orchestrates hook → context → taint → detect → policy → action; blocking logic (abort sink op, return safe error to app).
  8. rasp-telemetry — Detection events → SIEM/CEF export, Prometheus metrics, tracing spans, audit log.
  9. rasp-cli — TOML policy loader, hot-reload, CLI driver, integration smoke tests.
  10. rasp-bench — attack corpus (SQLi/cmdi/pathtraversal/SSRF/deser/XXE variants), benign corpus, latency overhead harness (criterion).

1.7 What Later Phases Unlock

  • Phase 6–7: Async-safe context propagation (tokio task-local, rayon thread-pool); full async fn sink interception.
  • Phase 8: Per-runtime adapters spec (JVM/JNI, .NET/CLR, Node N-API); trampoline ABI tests.
  • Phase 9: Advanced taint propagation (regex match, format string, base64 decode); taint cost profiling.
  • Phase 10: Virtual patching DSL; waiver API; OWASP CRS rule import.
  • Phase 11: IAST-mode passive observation (no blocking) with taint path export to DAST — PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register").
  • Phase 12: ML/AI confidence scoring layer — PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register").
  • Phase 13: Cross-service taint propagation via W3C traceparent headers — PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register").
  • Phase 14: Kernel-assisted sink interception (eBPF + uprobe) — PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register").

1.10 Language Rationale

Rust (edition 2021, MSRV 1.78):

  • Inline guard runs in every request path — zero-overhead abstractions, no GC pauses.
  • Memory-safe parsing of untrusted SQL/XML/serialized blobs without buffer overflows.
  • unsafe trampolines bounded and auditable; safe wrappers enforce invariants.
  • Thread-local / task-local context propagation without runtime overhead.
  • no_std-compatible core types for embedded adapter builds.
  • Strong type system enforces TaintedValue vs plain String at API boundaries.
  • Rejected: JVM/JDK agent (GC pauses, reflection overhead); C (memory unsafety in parser paths); Go (GC, goroutine scheduler interference with host); Python (interpreter overhead).

1.11 Required Prerequisites

Domain Depth needed
Systems programming FFI, trampolines, unsafe, linker scripts, ABI stability
Concurrency Thread-local storage, async/await, task-local values, lock-free channels
Networking HTTP/1.1 + HTTP/2 request parsing, URL normalization, DNS rebinding vectors
Security Injection attack taxonomy (SQLi, cmdi, pathtraversal, SSRF, deser, XXE, LDAP), taint analysis theory
Parser theory Tokenisation, AST construction, EBNF grammars for SQL/XML/LDAP
Observability Prometheus, OpenTelemetry, SIEM/CEF format, structured logging
Build tooling Cargo workspace, cbindgen, criterion benchmarking

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

  • Inline instrumentation theory: hook ABI design, trampoline construction, re-entrancy guards, safe interception without corrupting host call frames.
  • Taint analysis at runtime: source annotation, tag propagation through string ops, sink-time taint check — distinct from static taint (SAST) and network-level (WAF).
  • Injection attack mechanics: tokenisation-based SQLi detection (libinjection algorithm), shell metacharacter analysis, path-normalisation attacks, SSRF URL allowlist bypass, Java deserialization gadget chain recognition, XXE entity expansion, LDAP filter injection.
  • Request-scoped context propagation: thread-local vs async task-local, context lifecycle, isolation across concurrent requests, zero-copy capture of HTTP headers/body.
  • Fail-open/fail-closed engineering: policy propagation under error, bounded execution guarantees, panic isolation.
  • Overhead budgeting for inline guards: CPU budget per sink, early-exit on untainted args, hot-path profiling, cache-line cost of TaintMap.

2.2 Historical + Architectural Significance

  • RASP coined by Gartner ~2012 (Joseph Feiman); generalises runtime injection protection beyond WAF.
  • OpenRASP (Baidu, 2017) demonstrated production viability at web-scale (Java agent, millions of req/s).
  • Contrast Security / Sqreen / Imperva RASP validated the commercial model; Sqreen acquired by Datadog → Datadog ASM.
  • Represents the third generation of injection defense: WAF (network, project 338) → SAST (static) → RASP (runtime, this project).
  • Architecturally analogous to: database query interceptors, OS system call interposition (seccomp), JVM bytecode instrumentation (ASM/Javassist), eBPF uprobe — RASP synthesises all these patterns.
  • Teaches the innermost security loop of modern application security: detect at the exact moment of danger, not before (SAST false positives) or after (IDS/SIEM).

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

Muscle Exercise
API design under ABI stability pressure Hook ABI must be stable across adapter versions; versioned structs
Zero-copy parsing SQL tokeniser operating on &[u8] without allocation
Type-state machines TaintedValue vs String enforced at type level, not runtime check
Lock-free data structures Per-request TaintMap with bounded allocation, no global lock on hot path
Unsafe code auditing Trampolines, FFI callbacks, raw pointer manipulation, bounded unsafe blocks
Criterion-driven perf engineering Latency regression gates in CI for hook overhead
Corpus-driven testing Attack corpus drives detection coverage; benign corpus drives FP rate

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

  • Fail-open/fail-closed: policy with explicit error semantics propagated through every layer.
  • Virtual patching: rule deployed without redeploying app — RASP operationalises this cleanly.
  • Overhead SLA: p95 latency budget enforced as a CI gate; not a "nice to have."
  • Monitor before Block: standard rollout discipline (observe → tune → enforce); built into policy model.
  • Structured audit logging: every block decision logged with taint path + evidence — SOC/compliance requirement.
  • Hot-reload policy: SIGHUP / file-watcher reload without restarting app; atomically swap Arc<Policy>.
  • Defense-in-depth instrumentation: RASP is one layer; telemetry feeds upstream SIEM for correlation.

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Related project Connection
338 — Web Application Firewall WAF = network-edge; RASP = in-app runtime; complementary; compare context availability, FP rates, bypass vectors
280 — SQL Query Engine SQL tokeniser in rasp-detect mirrors query parsing in project 280; share EBNF grammar insights
270 — HTTP/1.1 + HTTP/2 Stack rasp-context captures HTTP request using same header parsing; reuse request model
300 — Async Runtime (tokio-style) Task-local context propagation in rasp-context requires deep async runtime understanding
310 — Compiler / Bytecode Weaving Hook ABI mirrors bytecode instrumentation (ASM weaving); trampoline = compiled JIT stub
320 — Distributed Tracing Correlation IDs, span propagation, W3C traceparent in rasp-context; telemetry integration
330 — Metrics / Observability Platform rasp-telemetry integrates with this; overhead metrics + detection events flow out
350 — Secret Scanner Complementary: RASP detects runtime injection; secret scanner detects static secrets at commit time

2.6 Calibration Value

  • Demonstrates mastery of runtime instrumentation — rare skill set bridging security, compilers, and systems.
  • Produces a concrete security product, not just infrastructure: tangible attack/block demo.
  • Forces precision in taint propagation: under-taint → false negatives; over-taint → false positives; calibration is the hard problem.
  • Attack corpus + benign corpus + latency harness → reproducible benchmarks reviewers can challenge.
  • Directly relevant to: AppSec platform teams, security product companies, cloud security vendors (AWS GuardDuty application sensor, GCP reCAPTCHA Enterprise, Cloudflare Workers security hooks).
  • Demonstrates ability to build opinionated, auditable unsafe code in Rust — senior-bar signal.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After completing this project the engineer holds:

  • Hook ABI design and trampoline construction (§13 — rasp-hook): can specify and implement a C-stable interception boundary, reason about re-entrancy guards, and audit every unsafe block with a justification comment.
  • Runtime taint analysis end-to-end (§16 — rasp-taint): understands source annotation, byte-range propagation through string ops, cost-budget arithmetic, and conservative fallback under budget exhaustion — distinct from both SAST taint and network-level heuristics.
  • Injection-attack tokenization (§17 — rasp-detect SQLi): can port or re-implement a libinjection-style fingerprint engine, reason about the token-diff approach, and calibrate confidence thresholds against a labeled corpus.
  • Context propagation in async runtimes (§14 — rasp-context): can explain why thread_local! breaks under tokio::spawn, implement task_local! scoping correctly, and write an isolation property test that catches cross-request taint leaks.
  • Overhead-SLA enforcement as a CI gate (§22 — rasp-bench): can set up a Criterion benchmark, define a regression budget, and diagnose a latency spike to its cause (allocation, branch misprediction, lock contention) using flamegraphs and perf.
  • Policy engine design with hot-reload (§21 — rasp-policy): can implement ArcSwap-based lock-free config swap, write the Monitor-purity invariant as a compile-time guarantee, and reason about waiver TTL expiry semantics.
  • Production observability for inline security agents (§24 — rasp-telemetry): can design a bounded async telemetry channel, write a CEF formatter with field escaping, and build a tamper-evident HMAC-chained audit log.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

protected application (instrumented runtime)  --hook ABI call at each dangerous sink-->
        |
  [rasp-hook] sink entry/exit interception (SQL exec, command exec, file open, outbound HTTP, deserialize)
        |  HookEvent (sink_type, args, context_id)
  [rasp-context] resolve per-request context (thread/async-local), HTTP request, call stack, correlation id
        |
  [rasp-taint] are sink args tainted? source (HTTP input) -> propagation -> sink reached?
        |  TaintedValue + taint path
  [rasp-detect] per-sink analyzer: SQLi (query tokenize + injected-syntax), cmd-injection, path-traversal,
        |        SSRF (URL allowlist), deserialization (gadget), XXE, LDAP/NoSQL -> Detection?
  [rasp-policy] Detection -> Action (Block / Monitor) by mode + rule + waiver (fail-open / fail-closed)
        |
  [rasp-protect] enforce: Block => abort the sink op + safe error to app; Allow => proceed
        |
  [rasp-telemetry] emit attack event (SIEM/CEF), metrics (overhead, blocks), audit log

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

Protected Application / Instrumented Runtime

  • The host process; can be JVM, Node.js, .NET CLR, Python CPython, or native Rust.
  • Per-runtime adapter (out-of-core) weaves hook calls at each sink: SQL driver execute(), Runtime.exec(), Files.open(), HttpClient.send(), ObjectInputStream.readObject(), JNDI lookup, DocumentBuilder.parse().
  • The adapter calls the hook ABI (rasp_hook_sink_entry, rasp_hook_sink_exit) via FFI/JNI/N-API.
  • Trust boundary: adapter code is trusted; sink args are untrusted payloads from user input.
  • Protocol boundary: hook ABI is a C-stable FFI boundary; extern "C" function signatures; versioned with a u32 abi_version handshake at rasp_init.

rasp-hook

  • Receives HookEvent at each sink; owns the call dispatch loop.
  • Maintains SinkRegistry: mapping SinkType → Vec<HookFn>; allows multiple interceptors per sink (e.g., audit + detect).
  • Enforces re-entrancy guard: if RASP itself calls a sink (e.g., telemetry writes a file), the inner call is passed through without re-intercepting.
  • Concurrency boundary: hook calls arrive from multiple threads/tasks; all state access goes through rasp-context (thread/task-local) — no shared mutable state in rasp-hook itself.
  • Trampoline: saves minimal register state, calls Rust hook fn, restores; bounded unsafe block, audited.
  • Failure mode: on any panic inside hook, catch_unwind catches; falls back to Policy.fail_mode; never propagates panic to host.

rasp-context

  • Owns RequestContext: id: CorrelationId, http: HttpInfo, taint_map: TaintMap, call_stack: Vec<Frame>, started: Instant.
  • Storage: thread_local! for synchronous/blocking apps; tokio::task_local! for async; adapter selects at init.
  • rasp_context_begin(http_info) called by adapter at request entry; rasp_context_end() at response exit.
  • Context isolation invariant: each request gets a fresh RequestContext; no fields shared across concurrent requests; TaintMap is per-context, not global.
  • HttpInfo captures: method, URL, headers (raw Vec<(String,String)>), body snapshot (up to max_body_capture bytes), remote IP.
  • call_stack: lightweight Vec<Frame> pushed/popped at hook entry/exit; used by rasp-detect for stack-context signals (e.g., call depth, framework frame detection).
  • Persistence boundary: RequestContext is in-process memory only; not persisted; telemetry extracts fields before drop.

rasp-taint

  • Consumes HttpInfo from rasp-context; annotates each HTTP field as a TaintedValue with TaintSource + TaintTag { source, byte_range }.
  • TaintMap: HashMap<ValueId, TaintedValue>; ValueId is a u64 handle assigned at source annotation.
  • Propagation policy: concat(a_tainted, b_clean) → tainted(merged_tags); slice(tainted, range) → tainted(adjusted_byte_range); format! with tainted arg → tainted output; propagation is field-level, not byte-level (bounded cost).
  • Taint soundness invariant: within configured ops set, propagation never silently drops taint; untracked ops (e.g., regex match, crypto hash) are documented as taint sinks that clear taint.
  • Overhead invariant: TaintMap lookup is O(1); propagation at string ops is O(tags) where tags ≤ MAX_TAGS_PER_VALUE (default 16); exceeds budget → mark TaintedValue::OverBudget → conservative (treat as tainted).
  • Sink-time check: given SinkArgs, resolves each arg's ValueId in TaintMap; returns Option<TaintedValue>.

rasp-detect

  • Per-sink analyzer dispatched by SinkType; receives (TaintedValue, SinkArgs, &RequestContext).
  • Short-circuit rule: if arg is not tainted, skip all analyzers → O(1) fast path; this is the primary overhead budget mechanism.
  • Analyzers:
    • SqliAnalyzer: tokenise query (libinjection algorithm port); detect injected syntax tokens (UNION, comment, stacked query, boolean blind); produce Detection { rule_id, confidence, evidence: TokenDiff }.
    • CmdInjectionAnalyzer: shell metacharacter scan (`; | && || $() `` etc.); argument boundary violation detection.
    • PathTraversalAnalyzer: normalize path; detect ../, null bytes, symlink bypass patterns; compare resolved path against allowed roots.
    • SsrfAnalyzer: parse URL; check against AllowedHostsPolicy; detect DNS rebinding patterns, loopback/link-local, internal RFC-1918 ranges, metadata endpoints (169.254.169.254).
    • DeserializationAnalyzer: check type name / class descriptor against gadget chain blocklist (ysoserial gadgets); detect java.lang.Runtime, ProcessBuilder, reflection chains.
    • XxeAnalyzer: scan DOCTYPE / ENTITY declarations; detect external entity references, SYSTEM/PUBLIC identifiers pointing to file:// or http://.
    • LdapInjectionAnalyzer: detect unescaped LDAP filter metacharacters (* ( ) \ NUL); NoSQL injection: detect $where, $gt, operator injection in JSON/BSON.
  • Each analyzer returns Option<Detection>; multiple analyzers can fire; highest-severity wins for policy dispatch, all are logged.
  • False-positive discipline: analyzers have configurable confidence_threshold; detections below threshold are downgraded to Monitor regardless of policy.

rasp-policy

  • Evaluates Vec<Detection> against Policy { mode: Monitor|Block, rules: Vec<Rule>, waivers: Vec<Waiver>, fail_mode: Open|Closed }.
  • Rule { id, sink, kind, action }: kind: Signature|Semantic; action: Allow|Block|Monitor.
  • Waiver: exempts specific (source_ip, rule_id, path) triple from blocking; time-bounded; signed.
  • fail_mode: Open → on RASP internal error, return Action::Allow; Closed → return Action::Block.
  • Decision is deterministic: same (Detection, Policy) → same Action; reproducible for audit.
  • Arc<Policy>: hot-reloaded atomically via ArcSwap; no lock on hot path; reload triggered by SIGHUP or file-watcher event.
  • Virtual patching: Rule with kind: Semantic and inline match expression applied before sink; deployed without redeploying app.

rasp-protect

  • The inline guard: called synchronously in the hook path; orchestrates the rasp-hook → rasp-context → rasp-taint → rasp-detect → rasp-policy → action pipeline.
  • Block path: returns Err(RaspError::Blocked { detection }) to the adapter; adapter translates to a safe application error (e.g., SQL driver throws SecurityException); the dangerous operation is NOT executed.
  • Allow path: returns Ok(()) to adapter; sink proceeds normally; overhead is the RASP check cost only.
  • Monitor path: returns Ok(()) but enqueues detection event to rasp-telemetry; no blocking; no app behavior change.
  • Monitor purity invariant: in Monitor mode, no call to Block path, no alteration of SinkArgs, no side effect on app execution.
  • Host-safety invariant: catch_unwind wraps entire protect path; on panic → fall back to fail_mode; never propagate panic to host stack.
  • Execution budget: configurable max_hook_duration_us (default 500µs); if exceeded, self-interrupts and falls back to fail_mode.

rasp-telemetry

  • Receives DetectionEvent via a bounded async channel (flume::bounded(4096)); non-blocking send from hot path (if channel full → drop event + increment events_dropped counter).
  • SIEM/CEF export: formats Detection as CEF:0 or JSON; writes to stdout/file/TCP socket; batched with configurable flush interval.
  • Prometheus metrics: rasp_hook_duration_us (histogram), rasp_detections_total (counter, labels: sink, rule, action), rasp_blocks_total, rasp_events_dropped_total, rasp_taint_map_size (gauge).
  • OpenTelemetry spans: each hook invocation creates a child span under the request trace; exports to OTLP collector.
  • Audit log: every Block decision logged with: correlation_id, sink, rule_id, action, evidence, taint_path, timestamp, request_url, source_ip; append-only, structured JSON.
  • Async queue boundary: telemetry consumer runs in a dedicated task/thread; decoupled from request path; backpressure via bounded channel.

3.3 Data Flow Summary

HTTP request arrives
    → adapter calls rasp_context_begin(HttpInfo)
    → rasp-taint annotates HTTP fields as TaintedValue, stores in RequestContext.taint_map
    → app processes request; calls SQL driver.execute(query_string)
    → adapter intercepts; calls rasp_hook_sink_entry(HookEvent{SqlQuery, args:[query_string], context_id})
    → rasp-protect dispatches:
        rasp-context: resolve RequestContext by context_id
        rasp-taint: lookup query_string ValueId → TaintedValue{tags:[HttpParam("q")]}
        rasp-detect: SqliAnalyzer(tainted_query) → Detection{SQLi, HIGH, evidence}
        rasp-policy: Detection + Policy → Action::Block
    → rasp-protect returns Err(Blocked) to adapter
    → adapter throws SecurityException (SQL never executes)
    → rasp-telemetry: DetectionEvent enqueued → CEF log + metrics
    → adapter calls rasp_context_end(); RequestContext dropped

3.4 Trust Boundaries

Boundary Description
Adapter ↔ RASP hook ABI C-stable FFI; versioned; adapter is trusted code
HTTP input ↔ taint annotation All HTTP fields are untrusted; annotated as tainted at boundary entry
Policy file ↔ policy engine File is operator-controlled; validated + schema-checked at load
Telemetry channel ↔ SIEM receiver One-way async export; no inbound commands via telemetry path
RequestContext ↔ concurrent requests Thread/task-local isolation; no cross-request access

3.5 Runtime Components

Component Concurrency model State
rasp-hook dispatcher Called from host thread/task No shared mutable state; reads SinkRegistry (Arc)
rasp-context store Thread-local or task-local Per-request; isolated
rasp-taint TaintMap Per-request HashMap In RequestContext; no global lock
rasp-detect analyzers Stateless per call Shared read-only rule tables (Arc)
rasp-policy evaluator Stateless per call Shared read-only Arc<Policy> via ArcSwap
rasp-protect inline guard Synchronous in hook path Orchestration only; no mutable shared state
rasp-telemetry consumer Dedicated async task/thread Bounded channel; append-only log

4. System Constraints

4.1 Constraints Table

Constraint Target Notes
Inline hook latency p50 ≤ 100 µs Measured at rasp-protect entry→exit for benign (untainted) args
Inline hook latency p95 ≤ 500 µs Tainted + detection path; includes tokenisation
Inline hook latency p99 ≤ 2 ms Worst-case: complex SQL + full SQLi analysis + policy eval
Overall request overhead (p95) ≤ 5% Net added latency vs baseline; measured by rasp-bench
Throughput degradation ≤ 5% RPS Benign corpus at peak load; CI regression gate
TaintMap memory per request ≤ 512 KB Bounded by max_taint_map_bytes; over limit → OverBudget mode
TaintMap entries per request ≤ 4096 MAX_TAINT_ENTRIES; configurable; prevents DoS via taint explosion
Tags per TaintedValue ≤ 16 MAX_TAGS_PER_VALUE; over limit → conservative merge
rasp-protect stack depth (hook path) ≤ 64 frames Prevents stack overflow in deeply nested call stacks
Max hook call duration (self-imposed) 500 µs max_hook_duration_us; exceeded → interrupt + fail_mode
Telemetry channel depth 4096 events flume::bounded(4096); overflow → drop + counter increment
Startup latency (rasp_init) ≤ 50 ms Policy load + taint table init + hook registration
Shutdown latency (rasp_shutdown) ≤ 500 ms Flush telemetry channel; drain in-flight events
Policy hot-reload latency ≤ 100 ms ArcSwap atomic swap; zero-downtime reload
Memory footprint (rasp engine, idle) ≤ 8 MB RSS Excluding per-request TaintMaps; base crate overhead
Max concurrent requests instrumented ≥ 10,000 Thread-local/task-local contexts; no global bottleneck
Max rules per Policy ≤ 10,000 Rule table lookup O(log n) via BTreeMap; tested at limit
CPU overhead at idle (no requests) ≈ 0% No background threads in core; telemetry consumer is event-driven
Hook ABI abi_version u32; current = 1 Bumped on breaking change; adapter checks at rasp_init
MSRV Rust 1.78 Required for task-local storage stability + ArcSwap compatibility
Target platforms x86_64-linux, aarch64-linux, x86_64-darwin, aarch64-darwin Primary CI matrix
OS / kernel min Linux 5.4+ / macOS 12+ Required for reliable thread-local destructors

4.2 Throughput + Scaling

  • Horizontal scaling: each app instance embeds its own RASP; no shared RASP process; scales with app.
  • Vertical scaling: TaintMap and analyzer are per-request; adding CPUs increases throughput linearly.
  • NUMA: per-request data structures allocated on calling thread's NUMA node; no cross-node contention.
  • Cache: TaintMap HashMap sized to fit in L2 cache for typical request (≤ 256 entries → ~16 KB); larger requests spill to L3.
  • Hot path: untainted-arg fast exit in rasp-taint reduces detector invocations to ~0% for benign args not derived from HTTP input.

4.3 Availability + Failure Budget

Metric Target
RASP-induced app crash rate 0% (host-safety invariant; catch_unwind on all hook paths)
RASP-induced false-positive block rate (benign corpus) ≤ 0.01% of requests
Detection coverage (attack corpus) ≥ 95% of labeled attack requests detected
Telemetry event loss rate (bounded channel) ≤ 0.1% under peak load
Policy reload availability 100% (ArcSwap; no request pause during reload)
RTO (RASP component restart) N/A — embedded; restarts with app
Fail-open default (brownfield) App continues on RASP internal error; 0% RASP-induced downtime

4.4 I/O + Storage

  • Audit log I/O: append-only structured JSON; async write via telemetry task; max write amplification: 1 log entry per blocked request (≤ 2 KB per entry).
  • Policy file: TOML/JSON; ≤ 10 MB; mmap-read at startup and reload.
  • No database: no persistent storage in core; telemetry is write-only to log/socket.
  • FD budget: ≤ 3 FDs per process (audit log file + metrics socket + policy file watch); does not consume app's FD budget materially.
  • Network: telemetry TCP export to SIEM; optional; max bandwidth ≤ 1 Mbps at 1000 blocks/s with CEF format.

4.5 Security Constraints

  • RASP engine code must pass cargo audit (no CVEs in deps) and cargo clippy --deny warnings in CI.
  • All unsafe blocks annotated with // SAFETY: justification; #![forbid(unsafe_code)] on all crates except rasp-hook (trampolines) and rasp-core (FFI types).
  • Attack corpus used in rasp-bench must include OWASP Top 10 (2021) injection variants; benign corpus must include high-volume framework traffic (Spring, Rails, Express patterns).
  • RaspError variants never expose internal taint paths in app-facing error messages (only in audit log); prevents oracle attacks via error messages.
  • Policy waivers must be time-bounded (TTL required) and signed (HMAC-SHA256 with operator key); no permanent anonymous waivers.
