Prometheus metric names Names frozen within MAJOR; new labels additive; removed metrics deprecated 1 MINOR before removal
JSON-L log fields Additive only within MAJOR; field renames preceded by 6-month dual-emit period

Storage / Config Schema Evolution

Artifact Policy
rasp.toml config fields Additive within MINOR; removed fields emit deprecation warning for 2 MINOR versions before error
Policy rule schema (TOML/JSON) Versioned by schema_version field; engine supports current and previous MAJOR schema version
HMAC policy signature Algorithm negotiated by hmac_algo field; SHA-256 default; migration: dual-sign during transition

API Stability (Rust Crate Public API)

Crate Stability
rasp-core types Stable; #[non_exhaustive] on enums; breaking layout change → MAJOR
rasp-hook adapter trait Stable; new optional trait methods get default impls (MINOR); removal → MAJOR
rasp-policy rule types Stable; new optional rule fields (MINOR); semantic behavior change → MAJOR
rasp-telemetry event types Stable; new optional event fields (MINOR)
Internal crates (rasp-taint, rasp-detect) No public API stability guarantee; used only by rasp-protect; version-locked in workspace

Rolling-Upgrade Guarantees

  • Old adapter (N) + new engine (N+1 MINOR): engine accepts adapter; old sinks continue to fire; new sinks not registered (no coverage regression, just no new sink coverage).
  • Old engine (N) + new adapter (N+1 MINOR): adapter checks engine ABI version at registration; if supported, registers; otherwise logs AdapterVersionMismatch and no-ops.
  • Cross-MAJOR upgrade: requires coordinated adapter + engine upgrade; documented migration guide per MAJOR release.
  • In-place rolling upgrade (Kubernetes): new pods with N+1 engine run alongside N; no shared mutable state → safe.

Deprecation Policy

Stage Duration Action
Deprecated ≥ 2 MINOR versions #[deprecated] attribute; WARN log at runtime if deprecated config used
Scheduled removal Announced in CHANGELOG Removal in next MAJOR; migration path documented
Emergency removal Security-only May be PATCH; disclosed in security advisory

Migration Guarantees

  • Config migration: rasp-cli migrate-config --from <old-version> rewrites rasp.toml to current schema; dry-run mode.
  • Policy migration: rasp-cli migrate-policy --from <old-schema-version> upgrades rule files; preserves rule IDs.
  • No database migration: RASP is stateless; no persistent runtime state to migrate.
  • Adapter migration: per-adapter migration guide published with each MAJOR; covers re-weaving instructions for target runtimes.

Testing & Verification Strategy

Unit Test Coverage Targets

Crate Target Coverage Priority Areas
rasp-core 95% type constructors, serialization round-trips, RaspError variants
rasp-taint 90% propagation rules, tag merging, cost-budget enforcement, sink-time lookup
rasp-detect 95% per-sink analyzer, signature matching, semantic scoring, edge inputs
rasp-policy 92% mode transitions, waiver matching, fail-open/closed paths
rasp-protect 88% orchestration paths, Block vs Monitor vs Allow dispatch, error fallback
rasp-context 90% store isolation, async/thread-local handoff, lifecycle teardown
rasp-hook 85% ABI dispatch, registration, multi-sink fanout
rasp-telemetry 80% CEF formatting, metric counters, audit log ordering

Per-Analyzer Unit Fixtures

  • SQLi (rasp-detect): 200+ canonical payloads (libinjection corpus + OWASP SQLi cheat sheet). Every tokenizer edge: stacked queries, out-of-band /**/, MySQL backtick identifiers, PostgreSQL $$ literals, Unicode lookalike bypass attempts.
  • Command injection: shell metachar fixtures (; | && || $() `` > < ), argument injection (option-as-value), whitespace/IFS manipulation.
  • Path traversal: ../, URL-encoded %2f, null byte \0, Windows UNC \\host\, double-encoded %252f, symlink-chain traversal.
  • SSRF: private RFC-1918 ranges, localhost aliases (0.0.0.0, [::], 127.1), DNS rebinding payloads, redirect chains, scheme bypass (file://, gopher://, dict://).
  • Deserialization: ysoserial gadget chain prefix heuristics (Java serialized magic ac ed, Pickle \x80\x04, PHP O:, Ruby Marshal \x04\x08).
  • XXE: external entity refs (SYSTEM, PUBLIC), parameter entities, DTD injection, SSRF-via-XXE.
  • LDAP/NoSQL: wildcard injection *, DN injection, MongoDB operator injection ($where, $regex).
  • Each fixture: (input, taint_source, expected_detection: bool, expected_severity, evidence_keywords).

Integration Tests — Full Pipeline

  • Hook → protect pipeline: synthetic HookEvent injected at rasp-hook; assert Action and emitted Detection at rasp-telemetry.
  • Context isolation: two concurrent synthetic requests (Tokio spawn + std::thread); assert taint from request A never observed in request B's RequestContext.
  • Fail-open path: poison rasp-detect with a panic-inducing rule; assert protected-app-side sees Allow (not panic propagation), host-safety invariant holds.
  • Fail-closed path: same setup with Policy { fail_mode: Closed }; assert Block returned, error logged.
  • Monitor purity: set Policy { mode: Monitor }; verify Action::Monitor returned for all attack fixtures, no abort invoked.
  • Block determinism: replay same (HookEvent, RequestContext, Policy) triple 1000x; assert identical Action + Detection every run.
  • Virtual patch roundtrip: load patch via rasp-policy, confirm matching HookEvent blocked without a full rasp-detect analyzer rule.

Differential Testing

  • libinjection cross-check: for every SQLi input, compare rasp-detect classification vs. libinjection C binding result; tolerate only documented divergences (no silent regressions).
  • WAF vs RASP divergence corpus: Project 338 (WAF) blocks vs RASP blocks on same payload set; document expected deltas (WAF sees HTTP payload only; RASP sees post-parse sink args).
  • Policy version diffs: replay identical corpus against two policy versions; flag any Block → Allow regressions as breaking changes requiring semver bump.

Property-Based Testing (proptest / quickcheck)

  • Taint propagation: ∀ (v: TaintedValue, op: StringOp) → result.tags ⊇ v.tags within cost budget.
  • Context isolation: ∀ concurrent (req_a, req_b) → req_a.taint_map ∩ req_b.taint_map = ∅.
  • Serialization roundtrip: ∀ (Detection, HookEvent, Rule, Policy) → serde_json round-trip idempotent.
  • Analyzer no-panic: ∀ arbitrary_bytes as sink_arg → analyzer returns Ok(None) or Ok(Some(Detection)), never panic.
  • Cost budget: ∀ (TaintedValue, n_operations) → tracking_cost ≤ configured_max_cost_budget.

Fuzzing (cargo-fuzz / AFL++)

Fuzz Target Input Invariant
fuzz_sql_analyzer arbitrary SQL string no panic, bounded alloc
fuzz_taint_propagate arbitrary (TaintedValue, StringOp) seq tags monotone, no OOM
fuzz_hook_dispatch arbitrary HookEvent bytes no panic, always returns Action
fuzz_xml_analyzer arbitrary XML/DTD bytes no panic, XXE detection correct
fuzz_deser_analyzer arbitrary bytes no panic, magic-byte detection stable
fuzz_policy_eval arbitrary (Detection, Policy) deterministic Action, no panic
fuzz_context_store concurrent read/write ops no UB, no taint leak
  • Minimum fuzz time: 24 h per target per release; crash corpus retained in repo under rasp-bench/fuzz-corpus/.
  • Coverage-guided: cargo-fuzz with libFuzzer; structured input variants via arbitrary crate.

Chaos and Fault Injection

  • Hook timeout injection: inject artificial delay in rasp-detect; assert overhead budget enforcement (circuit-break, fallback to fail-open/closed) within deadline.
  • OOM simulation: cap allocator at threshold; assert no panic propagates to host app; telemetry emits RaspError::ResourceExhausted.
  • Concurrent context corruption: deliberately race context writes across threads; assert context isolation invariant via loom model-checker runs.
  • Telemetry sink failure: block SIEM export; assert in-process audit log still written; no request blocked due to telemetry backpressure.
  • Policy reload mid-flight: hot-swap Policy during active requests; assert no request sees partial policy state; blocking decisions remain consistent.

Deterministic Replay

  • rasp-bench captures (HookEvent, RequestContext, Policy) snapshot per request (opt-in via env flag).
  • Replay harness: load snapshot, re-run full pipeline, assert identical Action + Detection + taint_path.
  • CI regression gate: replay golden snapshots from last 5 releases; any deviation fails build.

Soak Tests

  • 72-hour continuous run against benign corpus (real web traffic trace, replayed); monitor: p99 latency delta, RSS growth, detection false-positive count.
  • Pass criteria: p99 overhead ≤5%, RSS growth ≤2 MB/h, zero false-positives on benign corpus.

Performance Regression Gate

  • rasp-bench overhead harness runs on every PR merge; baseline stored in CI artifact store.
  • Threshold: >5% p95 latency increase vs baseline → PR blocked, requires explicit override + comment.
  • Tracked metrics: per-hook latency (ns), taint check cost (ns), full pipeline (µs), memory per request (bytes).

Conformance Validation

  • OWASP RASP test suite (when published): all attack categories must detect; false-negative budget ≤2% per category.
  • Internal conformance matrix: every SinkType × TaintSource pair has ≥1 positive and ≥1 negative fixture.

Compatibility Testing

  • Hook ABI compatibility: compile rasp-hook against each supported runtime adapter stub; run smoke test suite.
  • MSRV gate: CI builds with rustc 1.78.0 on every PR; no rustup override drift.
  • OS matrix: Linux x86-64 (primary), Linux aarch64, macOS aarch64 (dev); CI runs all three.

Build & Release Engineering

Reproducible Builds

  • All crates use locked Cargo.lock; cargo build --locked enforced in CI.
  • SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH set from git commit timestamp for deterministic outputs.
  • __DATE__/__TIME__-style nondeterminism prohibited; no std::time::SystemTime embedded in binary at build time.
  • Docker build images pinned to immutable digest (not tag); base image: rust:1.78-slim@sha256:<digest>.
  • Build reproducibility verified: two independent CI runs produce byte-identical rasp-protect shared library; verified with diffoscope.

Artifact Signing

  • Release binaries and .so/.dylib artifacts signed with sigstore/cosign (keyless, GitHub OIDC).
  • Signature attached as OCI manifest annotation; verification command documented in INSTALL.md.
  • GPG detached signatures (.asc) for tarball distribution; signing key fingerprint pinned in repo.
  • cargo-deny + cargo-auditable embeds dependency manifest in binary for post-deployment audit.

SBOM Generation

  • cargo-sbom generates CycloneDX 1.4 SBOM on every release build.
  • SBOM attached to GitHub Release as rasp-<version>-sbom.cdx.json.
  • SPDX 2.3 format also generated for enterprise toolchain compatibility.
  • SBOM includes: crate name, version, license (SPDX), source URL, checksum (SHA-256).
  • Automated SBOM diff on PR: flags new transitive dependencies requiring review.

CI/CD Topology

PR push → [lint: clippy --deny warnings] → [fmt: rustfmt --check]
       → [MSRV build: rustc 1.78] → [stable build] → [nightly build]
       → [unit tests: cargo test --workspace] → [property tests]
       → [fuzz smoke: 60s per target] → [bench regression gate]
       → [SBOM diff] → [cargo-deny (licenses + advisories)]
       → merge gate (all green required)

Merge to main → [release build --locked] → [reproducibility verify]
             → [artifact signing] → [SBOM publish] → [staging deploy]
             → [integration soak: 1h] → [promote to release channel]
  • CI provider: GitHub Actions; runners pinned to specific runner image versions.
  • Parallelism: unit tests split across 4 shards by crate; fuzz targets run in parallel jobs.
  • Cache: sccache for incremental compilation; cache keyed on Cargo.lock + rustc version hash.
  • Secrets: signing keys in GitHub OIDC; never stored as plain env vars.

Version Stamping

  • Version format: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH[-rc.N]; follows SemVer strictly.
  • Breaking hook ABI change → MAJOR bump; new SinkType or Detection field → MINOR; bug fix → PATCH.
  • Build metadata embedded: git rev-parse HEAD, build timestamp (UTC, ISO 8601), rustc version, target triple.
  • Accessible at runtime: rasp_core::build_info() -> BuildInfo { version, git_sha, rustc, target }.

Binary Provenance

  • SLSA Level 2 provenance: GitHub Actions generates provenance.json per artifact using slsa-github-generator.
  • Provenance uploaded to GitHub Release; verifiable with slsa-verifier verify-artifact.
  • Artifact checksums (SHA-256, SHA-512) published in SHA256SUMS / SHA512SUMS files, signed.

Dependency Auditing

  • cargo-audit runs on every CI push; RUSTSEC advisory database consulted; build fails on any high-severity advisory without explicit ignore + justification comment.
  • cargo-deny enforces: license allowlist (MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD-2-Clause, BSD-3-Clause); banned crates list; duplicate version policy (zero duplicates for rasp-* crates).
  • cargo-machete detects unused dependencies; CI fails on any unused dep.
  • Dependency update policy: dependabot weekly PRs; security patches merged within 48 h of advisory.

Supply-Chain Verification

  • cargo vet with mozilla/mozilla-vet audits for every transitive dependency; unaudited crates require explicit unaudited + justification entry.
  • cargo-crev proofs tracked for critical crypto/parsing crates (nom, serde, ring if used).
  • No build.rs scripts that download artifacts at build time; all vendored or via Cargo registry only.
  • Vendored dependencies (cargo vendor) used for air-gapped / regulated environments; vendor tree integrity verified with sha256sum.

Release Channels

Channel Trigger Stability Audience
nightly merge to main unstable, may break ABI internal CI, early adopters
beta tag vX.Y.Z-rc.N RC, ABI frozen integration partners
stable tag vX.Y.Z production all users
lts designated stable tag 12-month patch support regulated environments
  • Channel metadata distributed via GitHub Releases + OCI registry (ghcr.io/rasp/rasp-protect).
  • LTS backport policy: security fixes only; no feature backports.

Release Automation

  • cargo-release orchestrates: version bump, CHANGELOG.md update (keep-a-changelog format), tag, push, GitHub Release creation.
  • Release checklist enforced as GitHub Action: SBOM attached, signatures verified, provenance uploaded, cargo-audit clean, bench baseline updated.
  • Rollback procedure: previous release artifact retained; documented rollback runbook in ops/ROLLBACK.md.

Performance Engineering

Benchmarking Strategy

  • Harness: rasp-bench crate; uses criterion for statistical micro-benchmarks + custom overhead harness for end-to-end pipeline.
  • Benchmark taxonomy:
Benchmark Granularity Goal
bench_hook_dispatch per-sink entry/exit hook overhead baseline (ns)
bench_taint_check tainted vs untainted args short-circuit effectiveness
bench_sql_analyzer per SQL string tokenizer throughput
bench_context_lookup thread-local vs task-local store access cost
bench_policy_eval per Detection rule matching latency
bench_full_pipeline end-to-end per request total RASP overhead (µs)
bench_benign_throughput RPS on benign corpus false-overhead floor
bench_attack_throughput RPS on attack corpus detection capacity
  • Statistical rigor: criterion runs minimum 100 iterations; reports mean ± std-dev, p50/p95/p99; outlier detection enabled.
  • Baseline storage: JSON benchmark results committed to rasp-bench/baselines/<git-sha>.json; regression gate compares against HEAD~1 baseline.

Profiling Methodology

  • CPU profiling: perf record -g --call-graph dwarf on Linux; cargo-flamegraph wraps it for Rust symbol demangling.
  • Sampling rate: 99 Hz (avoids lockstep with scheduler); profile duration ≥ 30 s under representative load.
  • Annotated hotspot workflow: perf annotate to instruction level; identify inlining failures, branch mispredicts, cache misses.
  • Allocation profiling: dhat (Valgrind) for heap allocation site analysis; heaptrack for allocation rate over time; cargo-dhat integration for in-process profiling.
  • Lock contention: perf lock or mutrace; target: zero contention on per-request hot path (all shared state behind Arc<RwLock> with read-side dominance).

Flamegraph Workflow

1. cargo build --release -p rasp-bench
2. cargo flamegraph --bin rasp-bench -- --bench full_pipeline --duration 30
3. open flamegraph.svg; identify:
   - rasp_detect::* width (should be <40% of RASP frame)
   - rasp_taint::* width (should be <20%)
   - context resolution (should be <5%)
   - hook dispatch overhead (should be <10%)
4. If rasp_detect widening: profile sub-analyzer, look for regex/alloc hotspots
5. Differential flamegraph (inferno-diff) between two commits for regression triage
  • Flamegraph artifacts attached to CI on benchmark-regression failures.
  • inferno (Rust-native flamegraph renderer) preferred over perl/flamegraph.pl.

Tracing Methodology

  • Instrumentation: tracing crate with per-span fields: sink_type, context_id, taint_hit: bool, action.
  • Sampling: 1-in-1000 requests in production tracing (configurable); 100% in bench/test.
  • Span hierarchy:
request [context_id]
  └─ hook_entry [sink_type, args_len]
       └─ context_resolve [store_type: thread|task]
       └─ taint_check [tainted: bool, tag_count]
       └─ detect [analyzer, confidence]
       └─ policy_eval [action, rule_id]
       └─ telemetry_emit [event_type]
  • Export: OTLP to local Jaeger for dev; production → vendor OTLP endpoint.
  • Overhead of tracing itself measured: target ≤50 ns per disabled span (cold path), ≤200 ns per enabled span.

Allocator / Lock / Cache / Syscall Analysis

Concern Tool Target
Heap alloc per request dhat, heaptrack ≤4 allocations on benign (untainted) path
Lock hold time mutrace, perf lock zero contention on per-request path
L1/L2 cache miss rate perf stat -e cache-misses <5% on hot taint-check path
Syscall count per request strace -c (dev only) 0 additional syscalls on benign path
Context switch rate perf stat -e cs no increase vs uninstrumented baseline
  • Thread-local storage access: verify no __tls_get_addr call overhead via perf; use #[thread_local] static where possible.
  • Avoid Mutex on hook hot path; prefer RwLock (read-biased) for shared policy; per-request RequestContext is never shared.

Throughput Targets

Scenario Target Measurement
Benign (untainted args) ≤5% RPS reduction vs baseline wrk / hey load test
Attack (tainted, analyzer triggered) ≤15% RPS reduction load test with attack corpus
SQLi detection throughput ≥500 K queries/s single-core bench_sql_analyzer
Full pipeline (benign) ≤5 µs p95 added latency bench_full_pipeline
Full pipeline (attack, block) ≤50 µs p95 added latency bench_full_pipeline (attack)
Policy eval ≤500 ns p99 bench_policy_eval
Taint check (untainted, short-circuit) ≤100 ns p99 bench_taint_check

Phase-Gated Performance Targets (M-Table)

Every number below is carried forward from the Throughput Targets table above and the §4.1 Constraints Table — no new numbers invented. Phase Gate names the phase whose deliverable first makes the metric measurable/enforceable end-to-end.

ID Metric Target Phase Gate
M1 Inline hook latency p50 (benign, untainted args) ≤ 100 µs Phase 1 — Hook / instrumentation framework
M2 Inline hook latency p95 (tainted + detection path) ≤ 500 µs Phase 4 — Taint tracking core
M3 Inline hook latency p99 (complex SQLi + full policy eval) ≤ 2 ms Phase 9 — Policy + enforcement
M4 Benign-path RPS overhead vs uninstrumented baseline ≤ 5% RPS reduction Phase 10 — Performance + low-overhead
M5 Attack-path RPS overhead (analyzer triggered) ≤ 15% RPS reduction Phase 10 — Performance + low-overhead
M6 SQLi analyzer throughput, single core ≥ 500K queries/s Phase 5 — SQL injection detection
M7 Full pipeline added latency, benign (p95) ≤ 5 µs Phase 10 — Performance + low-overhead
M8 Full pipeline added latency, attack/block (p95) ≤ 50 µs Phase 9 — Policy + enforcement
M9 Policy evaluation latency (p99) ≤ 500 ns Phase 9 — Policy + enforcement
M10 Taint check latency, untainted short-circuit (p99) ≤ 100 ns Phase 4 — Taint tracking core
M11 Heap allocations per request (benign path) ≤ 4 allocations Phase 10 — Performance + low-overhead
M12 Per-request peak memory (RequestContext + TaintMap) ≤ 32 KB Phase 2 — Execution context engine

Tail-Latency Analysis

  • P99/P999 focus: criterion output inspected for distribution shape; bimodal distributions indicate analyzer branch divergence.
  • Latency heatmap: HdrHistogram used in overhead harness; export HDR files, render with hdrlog2gnuplot.
  • Outlier attribution: any p999 >10× p95 triggers investigation; common causes: analyzer first-call JIT effect (not applicable in Rust, but LLVM cold code paths), OS scheduling jitter, TLB pressure on large context maps.
  • Deadline enforcement: rasp-protect wraps rasp-detect in bounded execution (configurable timeout, default 2 ms); exceeding deadline → log + fallback to fail-open/closed; prevents tail-latency amplification from slow analyzers.

Memory Profiling

  • Per-request allocation budget: RequestContext + TaintMap target ≤32 KB peak per request.
  • Taint cost budget enforced at runtime: TaintMap::cost_guard() aborts further tracking when budget exhausted; logged as RaspError::TaintBudgetExceeded.
  • RSS growth soak: 72-hour run baseline RSS vs end RSS; target ≤2 MB/h growth (indicates no leak).
  • valgrind --tool=massif for heap snapshot; ms_print for peak allocation sites.
  • cargo-dhat in test suite: assert per-request allocation counts in bench_full_pipeline.

Scalability Analysis

Bottleneck Inventory

Bottleneck Crate Manifestation Mitigation
Taint map size rasp-taint memory growth per long request per-request cost budget cap
Analyzer regex compilation rasp-detect first-call latency spike lazy_static / once_cell pre-compile at startup
Policy rule scan rasp-policy O(n_rules) per Detection sorted rule vec + binary search on rule_id prefix
Context store contention rasp-context async task-local vs thread-local mismatch separate code paths; benchmark both
Telemetry channel backpressure rasp-telemetry event drop under load bounded channel; drop-on-overflow with counter
Hook registration lock rasp-hook startup contention one-time write at init; read-only at runtime

Scaling Ceilings

  • Per-process ceiling: RASP is in-process; scales with app process/thread count; no inter-process coordination on hot path.
  • Taint map ceiling: unbounded taint tags per value → mitigated by max_tags_per_value (default 64) and max_cost_budget; beyond ceiling: taint tracking degrades gracefully (logs, does not crash).
  • Concurrent request ceiling: limited by RequestContext store size (thread-count for thread-local, task-count for async); no global bottleneck; scales horizontally with app concurrency model.
  • Rule count ceiling: O(n_rules) scan acceptable to ~1000 rules; beyond: index by SinkType (reduces to O(n_rules_per_sink)); test matrix at 10, 100, 1000, 10000 rules.
  • Telemetry throughput ceiling: bounded channel (default 8192 events); at saturation events dropped + rasp_telemetry_dropped_total counter incremented; SIEM export is async, non-blocking on hook path.

Horizontal Scaling

  • RASP is embedded per-app-instance; horizontal scaling = more app instances = more RASP instances; no shared state between instances.
  • Policy distribution: policy file loaded at startup from shared volume or remote config endpoint (rasp-policy supports hot reload via file-watch + inotify/kqueue); no per-instance policy divergence in steady state.
  • Attack event aggregation: each instance emits to SIEM independently; aggregation happens at SIEM layer; no in-process fan-in required.
  • No leader election, no distributed lock; all coordination is read-only config pull.

Vertical Scaling

  • More cores → more concurrent requests handled → proportional RASP overhead (no shared lock on hot path).
  • Memory: each concurrent request adds RequestContext (~32 KB peak); at 10 K concurrent requests → ~320 MB additional RSS; within typical server headroom.
  • CPU: each request adds ≤5 µs RASP overhead; at 10 K RPS → ≤50 ms CPU/s = ≤5% of 1 core; scales linearly with request rate.
  • NUMA: RequestContext allocated on requesting thread's NUMA node (thread-local); no cross-NUMA access on hot path; telemetry channel writer/reader may cross NUMA — acceptable (async, non-blocking).

Sharding / Partitioning

  • No data sharding required: RASP is stateless per request (context is request-scoped, discarded at request end).
  • Policy sharding: large rule sets partitioned by SinkType at load time; each SinkType has its own Vec<Rule> slice; O(1) partition lookup.
  • Taint map partitioning: TaintMap keyed by input field name; no global taint map; per-request only.
  • Audit log partitioning: if writing to local file, rotate by date + size; no cross-instance coordination.

Replication Pressure

  • No replication within RASP itself; SIEM is the replication target.
  • Policy config replication: central config store → each app instance; at 1000 instances × 100 KB policy → 100 MB fan-out per policy update; staggered reload (jittered 0–60 s) prevents thundering herd.
  • Attack event replication: each event ~1 KB; at 100 events/s × 1000 instances → 100 MB/s to SIEM; ensure SIEM ingestion capacity before deployment at scale.

Coordination Overhead

  • Zero coordination on request hot path: all decisions are local (in-process, in-memory).
  • Policy reload coordination: file-watch signal → atomic pointer swap (ArcSwap or parking_lot::RwLock with short write hold); readers always see consistent snapshot.
  • No distributed consensus, no service mesh call, no remote lookup on hot path — fundamental RASP design requirement (contrast: centralized WAF rule engine).

Network Fanout

  • Inbound (to RASP): none on hot path; policy config is pulled at startup + on file change.
  • Outbound (from RASP): telemetry events to SIEM; batched (default 100 events or 1 s, whichever first); non-blocking send to channel; async exporter thread handles network I/O.
  • SSRF analyzer requires URL allowlist config: loaded at startup, no per-request DNS or network call; allowlist is in-memory HashSet<CidrBlock> + HashSet<DomainPattern>.
  • No back-channel to RASP vendor on hot path; all communication is app-originated telemetry push.

Scheduler Saturation

  • Async runtime (Tokio): RASP adds per-task overhead ≤5 µs; at 100 K tasks/s → 500 ms CPU/s = 50% of 1 core at extreme load; accept as upper bound.
  • Thread-pool (Rayon/std): RASP hooks execute on calling thread; no RASP-owned thread pool on hot path; telemetry exporter runs on dedicated background thread.
  • Scheduler saturation symptoms: telemetry channel backpressure (event drops), taint budget exhaustion; both degrade gracefully without blocking app threads.
  • tokio::task::yield_now() not called in RASP hot path; analyzers are synchronous and bounded; no cooperative scheduling yield required.

Storage Growth

  • Audit log: rotating file, default max 1 GB / 30 days; configurable.
  • Fuzz corpus: grows monotonically; prune to unique coverage-increasing inputs; target ≤500 MB per target after pruning.
  • Benchmark baselines: one JSON per commit; prune to last 90 commits; ≤50 MB total.
  • Attack event archive: SIEM-side concern; RASP produces events, does not store them locally beyond the bounded telemetry channel.

Metadata Scaling

  • Rule metadata: Policy struct held in memory; 10000 rules × ~200 bytes/rule = ~2 MB; acceptable.
  • Taint tag metadata: Vec<TaintTag> capped at max_tags_per_value (64 default) × sizeof(TaintTag) (~32 bytes) = ~2 KB per tainted value; bounded.
  • Context metadata: RequestContext ~32 KB peak; 10 K concurrent requests = 320 MB; monitor RSS in soak test.
  • Telemetry metrics: rasp-telemetry uses atomic counters (AtomicU64); no dynamic allocation for metric updates; Prometheus scrape is pull-based, no push overhead on hot path.

Mental Models & First Principles

The compressed insights an expert in runtime application self-protection holds. Each predicts a bug class. Every cited § resolves to a real heading.

MM1. A RASP sees intent at the sink; a WAF sees bytes at the edge. Detection runs inside the process at the moment the dangerous operation executes, with the fully-assembled query/command and its data provenance in hand. Predicts: far lower false positives than pattern-matching the raw request. Bug without it: building a RASP that re-matches request patterns like a WAF — you inherit the WAF's blindness to post-decode, post-deserialize, and ORM-built payloads. Applied: §8 (sink interception), §19 (SQLi at sink), Comparative Architecture Table.

MM2. Taint is a provenance label that flows with data, not a scan you run. A TaintedValue carries byte-range tags from source (HTTP input) through string operations to sink. Predicts: you can answer "did attacker-controlled bytes reach this exact position?" Bug without it: re-scanning whole strings for "bad patterns" at the sink — O(n) per call and semantically blind to provenance. Applied: §16 (taint core), §8.7 (TaintMap Data Model), P-taint principles.

MM3. A security tool that crashes the app is worse than the attack it stops. Every hook path is wrapped in catch_unwind and defaults fail-open in brownfield deployments. Predicts: the 0% RASP-induced crash invariant (§4.3). Bug without it: a panic in a taint propagator takes down the host request — you have built an availability vulnerability. Applied: §4.3 (host-safety budget), §13 (hook trampolines), §21 (fail-open vs fail-closed).

MM4. Injection = tainted data reaching a syntactic keyword position. SQLi is not "input contains a quote"; it is "attacker bytes altered the parse tree of the query." Predicts: high SQLi precision via tokenize-and-diff (libinjection-style). Bug without it: alerting on the mere presence of taint or of metacharacters → false positives on legitimate quoted data. Applied: §17 (SQLi tokenization), §18 (command/path), §8 domain model.

MM5. Monitor mode must be observationally pure. In monitor mode the engine may compute, score, and log, but must never alter app control flow or output. Predicts: safe staged rollout (monitor → block). Bug without it: a "monitor" mode that occasionally blocks or mutates → operators lose trust and disable the agent. Applied: §21 (monitor vs block), P-policy principles, I-invariants.

MM6. Overhead is a budget spent per sink call — protect the fast path. Untainted arguments must take an inline fast exit before any analyzer runs. Predicts: the ≤ 5% request overhead target (§4.1). Bug without it: running full detection on every argument regardless of taint → linear blow-up and missed SLA. Applied: §4.1 (overhead budget), §22 (performance), §16 (untainted fast path).

MM7. Context propagation across async boundaries needs task-local, not thread-local. Under tokio::spawn a request hops threads; thread_local! silently loses the RequestContext. Predicts: correct per-request isolation in async runtimes. Bug without it: cross-request taint leaks where request A's taint is checked against request B's sink. Applied: §14 (execution context engine), §11 Core Schema (RequestContext).

MM8. Bound everything an attacker can inflate. TaintMap bytes, entries, tags-per-value, telemetry channel depth, and hook stack depth all have hard caps with conservative-fallback modes. Predicts: resilience to taint-explosion DoS. Bug without it: an attacker sends input that fans out into millions of taint tags → OOM or unbounded latency. Applied: §4.1 (limits table), §16 (OverBudget mode), §20 (correlation/dedup).

MM9. Evasion is a normalization problem: decode before you match. URL/hex/unicode/double-encoding and SQL comment/whitespace tricks must be canonicalized before analysis. Predicts: anti-bypass robustness (§23). Bug without it: matching raw %2527 instead of the decoded ' → trivial bypass. Applied: §23 (evasion resistance), §17/§18 (normalization steps).

MM10. The hook ABI is a versioned contract between agent and runtime. abi_version is checked at rasp_init; a mismatch must refuse to load, not run with undefined behaviour. Predicts: safe upgrades across agent/runtime skew. Bug without it: a new agent against an old runtime adapter reads misaligned HookEvent structs → memory corruption. Applied: §4.1 (abi_version), §13 (hook ABI), Compatibility & Versioning.

Comparative Architecture Table

This design (inline in-process hooks + byte-range taint + sink-time tokenization + monitor/block policy) versus four real runtime-protection products, with a WAF as the out-of-band contrast. Real systems only.

Dimension This blueprint (rasp) Contrast Security OpenRASP (Baidu) Datadog ASM / Sqreen ModSecurity (WAF, contrast)
Instrumentation Rust hook ABI + trampolines JVM/.NET bytecode agent language hooks (PHP/Java/Python) tracer-integrated instrumentation reverse-proxy / module at edge
Taint tracking byte-range TaintedValue full dataflow taint hook-point checks (limited taint) per-span taint annotation none (request pattern match)
Detection model tainted-reaches-keyword + tokenize dataflow + rules parameter/stack signatures rules + ddwaf patterns regex rule sets (CRS)
Enforcement monitor/block, virtual patch block at sink block at hook block via WAF integration block at edge
Context task-local per request agent-managed per-request trace/span context request-scoped at proxy
Overhead model ≤ 5% (fast-path untainted) moderate (agent JIT) low–moderate tracer overhead edge latency only
Blind spots requires runtime adapter per language JVM/.NET focus per-language hook coverage WAF-leaning patterns no app context (post-decode)

Contrast Security instruments JVM/.NET bytecode to perform full dataflow taint inside the runtime; it wins for managed-runtime enterprises wanting deep taint without source changes. This blueprint targets a Rust hook ABI with explicit byte-range taint to keep overhead bounded and the trust boundary auditable, accepting that each language needs a runtime adapter.

OpenRASP (Baidu, open-source) places hooks at known dangerous call sites and checks parameters/stack signatures rather than maintaining full taint; it wins for quick deployment on PHP/Java/Python web stacks. We chose explicit taint propagation (MM2) because signature-at-sink alone produces more false positives on legitimately-quoted data (MM4).

Datadog ASM / Sqreen integrates protection into the APM tracer with per-span annotation and a ddwaf rule engine; it wins where an org already runs Datadog tracing and wants security as another signal. We reject the WAF-leaning pattern model for core detection because it relies on network-edge patterns rather than pure sink semantics (the explicit critique in §5 R3).

ModSecurity (the WAF contrast) matches regexes against the HTTP request at a reverse proxy; it wins as a language-agnostic first line of defence with zero app changes. We reject it as the core model because it cannot see the assembled query, post-deserialization objects, or ORM-built SQL — exactly the contexts where RASP's sink-time view (MM1) is decisive.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

Numbers an expert computes before designing, each derived from §4. Assume a baseline request of 20 ms server time and ~10 instrumented sink calls/request.

  • C1 — Per-request hook budget. 5% of a 20 ms request = 1 ms added latency budget (§4.1). Across 10 sink calls that is 100 µs/call average — exactly the p50 hook target; only the few tainted calls may spend up to the 500 µs p95.
  • C2 — TaintMap memory at peak concurrency. 512 KB/request × 10 000 concurrent (§4.1) = 5.1 GB worst case; this is why the per-request cap and OverBudget fallback (MM8) exist — without the cap, taint memory, not detection, is the failure mode.
  • C3 — Taint-tag fan-out ceiling. 4096 entries × 16 tags (§4.1) = 65 536 tags maximum per request; beyond it the engine does a conservative merge rather than growing — bounding the taint-explosion DoS.
  • C4 — Telemetry drain rate. Channel depth 4096 (§4.1); at 1 000 blocks/s an event-driven consumer must drain in ≤ 4.096 s of buffer — comfortably ahead of the ≤ 0.1% loss budget (§4.3) given async export.
  • C5 — SIEM export bandwidth. 1 000 blocks/s × ~125 B CEF record = 1 Mbps (§4.4), confirming the stated cap; verbose JSON (~2 KB/entry) would be 16× and is therefore reserved for the local audit log, not the wire.
  • C6 — File-descriptor footprint. 3 FDs/process (audit log + metrics socket + policy watch, §4.4); negligible against the host app's budget — a deliberate non-intrusion property.
  • C7 — Policy lookup cost. 10 000 rules in a BTreeMap (§4.1) ⇒ ≈ log₂(10 000) ≈ 14 comparisons per sink classification; sub-microsecond, off the latency critical path.
  • C8 — Idle footprint. ≤ 8 MB RSS idle (§4.1) plus live per-request TaintMaps; at 1 000 concurrent typical requests (~16 KB L2-resident map each, §4.2) that is ~16 MB of taint state — fits comfortably.
  • C9 — Detection-quality budget. ≥ 95% attack-corpus coverage at ≤ 0.01% benign false-positive rate (§4.3): at 1 M requests/day with 0.01% FP that is ≤ 100 false blocks/day — the operational ceiling that makes block-mode tolerable.
  • C10 — Throughput overhead. ≤ 5% RPS degradation (§4.1): a service doing 2 000 RPS may drop to ≥ 1 900 RPS under instrumentation — the CI regression gate fails the build below this.
  • C11 — Hot-reload window. ArcSwap atomic swap ≤ 100 ms (§4.1) with zero request pause: in-flight requests keep the old Arc<Policy>; new requests see the new one — no lock, no drain.
  • C12 — Stack-depth guard. ≤ 64 frames on the hook path (§4.1): bounds re-entrancy and prevents the agent itself from overflowing a deep app call stack.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Each requires synthesizing ≥ 2 sections.

  1. Why does a RASP produce fewer false positives than a WAF on the same SQLi payload, and what does it require that a WAF cannot have? (MM1 + MM4 + Comparative Architecture)
  2. How is "tainted data reached a SQL keyword position" computed, and why is mere taint presence insufficient to alert? (MM2 + MM4 + §17)
  3. What guarantees a panic inside a taint propagator cannot crash the host request? (MM3 + §4.3 + §13)
  4. Why does thread_local! break request context under tokio, and what replaces it? (MM7 + §14 + §11)
  5. Trace one tainted HTTP parameter from source annotation to a blocked SQL sink, naming each crate and latency budget. (§3 + §4.1 + §16 + §19)
  6. How does the engine stay within a 5% overhead budget when most arguments are untainted? (MM6 + Capacity C1 + §22)
  7. What attacker behaviour does the TaintMap byte/entry/tag cap defend against, and what is the fallback? (MM8 + Capacity C2/C3 + §4.1)
  8. Why must monitor mode be observationally pure, and what operational failure follows if it is not? (MM5 + §21 + §4.3)
  9. How are double-encoded payloads defeated, and at what stage of the pipeline? (MM9 + §23 + §17)
  10. What does the hook abi_version protect against, and what is the failure mode of a mismatch? (MM10 + §4.1 + Compatibility & Versioning)
  11. Why is full SQL/edge pattern matching rejected as the core detection model despite WAF ubiquity? (Comparative Architecture + Alternative Architectures + MM1)
  12. How is the ≤ 0.01% benign false-positive budget connected to whether block mode is deployable? (Capacity C9 + §4.3 + §21)
  13. What is the memory ceiling at 10 000 concurrent instrumented requests, and which design choice bounds it? (Capacity C2 + MM8 + §4.1)
  14. How does policy hot-reload achieve zero request pause, and what data structure enables it? (MM5 + Capacity C11 + §21)
  15. Why is eBPF/kernel sink interception deferred rather than chosen as the primary mechanism? (Alternative Architectures + Deferred Features Register + MM1)
  16. How does the SIEM export stay within 1 Mbps while the audit log keeps full detail? (Capacity C5 + §4.4 + §24)
  17. What distinguishes RASP taint from SAST taint, and why can't SAST replace it? (MM2 + Alternative Architectures + §2)
  18. How does sink-time correlation/dedup prevent one attack from emitting many alerts? (§20 + MM8 + §24)
  19. What is the blast radius if the RASP engine itself has a bug on the hook path, and how is it contained? (MM3 + §4.3 + Failure Modes & Recovery)
  20. Why is virtual patching possible in this architecture, and how does it differ from blocking? (§21 + MM1 + Compatibility & Versioning)

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Alt-A — Network WAF (edge pattern matching).

client --HTTP--> [ WAF: regex rules on request ] --(pass)--> app

Wins when: you need language-agnostic protection with zero app changes and a fast first line of defence (CDN/edge). Embodied by: ModSecurity + OWASP CRS, Cloudflare WAF. Rejected (as core) because: the WAF cannot see the assembled query, deserialized objects, or ORM-built SQL, so it trades precision for placement (MM1); kept as a complementary outer layer, not the detection core.

Alt-B — Static analysis (SAST) at build time.

source --> [ taint analysis: source->sink paths ] --> findings (pre-deploy)

Wins when: you want to eliminate vulnerability classes before deployment with no runtime overhead. Embodied by: CodeQL, Semgrep, commercial SAST. Rejected because: SAST has no runtime values (is this request's input actually attacker-controlled?), over-reports on infeasible paths, and cannot cover dynamically-loaded or reflective code; it is a build-time complement, not a runtime protector.

Alt-C — Kernel-level syscall interception (eBPF/uprobe).

app --syscalls--> [ eBPF programs at exec/connect/openat ] --> allow/deny

Wins when: you want one language-agnostic sensor below the runtime with no in-process code. Embodied by: Falco, Tetragon. Rejected (deferred) because: the kernel sees execve/connect but not which SQL string or which deserialized class — it lacks application-level semantics (MM1); captured as a future enhancement in the Deferred Features Register.

Alt-D — IAST (passive, test-time instrumentation).

QA traffic --> [ instrumented app reports taint paths ] --> findings (no blocking)

Wins when: you want to surface vulnerabilities during testing/CI without any production enforcement risk. Embodied by: Contrast Assess, Seeker. Rejected (deferred) as the primary mode because: IAST observes but does not protect production; this blueprint's purpose is inline enforcement (monitor/block), with IAST-style passive export kept in the Deferred Features Register.

Deferred Features Register

Every PhaseDeferred("Deferred Features Register") anchor resolves here. Each row is out of the Phase-0–13 scope.

Feature Anchor Building Phase (if undeferred) Un-defer Trigger
IAST passive taint export to DAST this Register row New passive-mode in rasp-taint Customers want test-time vulnerability discovery (Alt-D)
ML/AI confidence scorer this Register row New rasp-ml scoring stage Rule/heuristic confidence proves insufficient on real corpora
Cross-service W3C traceparent taint propagation this Register row New distributed-context layer Microservice deployments need taint across service hops
eBPF/uprobe kernel sink hooks this Register row Kernel-sensor adapter Language without a runtime adapter must be covered (Alt-C)
GUI dashboard this Register row Separate front-end project Operators need a visual console beyond SIEM/metrics
Auto-remediation / code patching this Register row New remediation engine Org wants automated fix suggestions, not just blocking

Glossary

Term Definition
RASP Runtime Application Self-Protection — security instrumentation embedded inside the running application process; intercepts dangerous operations at their call sites with full semantic context unavailable to network-edge defenses.
Sink A dangerous operation site where untrusted data produces harm if injected: SQL exec, OS command exec, file open, outbound HTTP, deserialization, LDAP query, XML parse. Represented as SinkType enum variant.
Source Origin of untrusted input entering the trust boundary: HttpParam, HttpHeader, HttpBody, HttpPath, Cookie — enumerated in TaintSource.
Taint tracking Data-flow analysis at runtime: each byte of untrusted input is tagged at ingress (TaintTag { source, byte_range }); tags propagate through string operations; checked at every sink.
TaintedValue Value paired with its set of TaintTags — TaintedValue { value, tags: Vec<TaintTag> }. Propagation preserves tags through concat, slice, format.
TaintTag Minimal provenance record: { source: TaintSource, byte_range: Range<usize> } — records which bytes of the final value originated from which HTTP field.
Taint propagation Rule set by which tags flow through string operations (concat → union, slice → sub-range, format → mapped tags); cost-bounded to keep overhead within budget.
Taint soundness (bounded) Invariant: no taint drop within the configured operation budget; operations exceeding budget are conservatively treated as fully tainted; untracked ops documented.
Hook A call intercept registered at a sink call site; the protected runtime invokes rasp_hook_entry(event) / rasp_hook_exit(result) at each instrumented site.
Hook ABI The C-compatible function signature boundary between the instrumented runtime (any language) and the Rust RASP engine — defined in rasp-hook; enables language-agnostic adapters.
Trampoline Low-level thunk that redirects control from the original sink implementation to the RASP hook entry point, then conditionally returns to the original; must be async-signal-safe.
Instrumentation adapter Per-runtime shim (JVM agent, .NET profiler, Node.js monkey-patch) that installs trampolines and emits HookEvents into the RASP engine via the hook ABI; specified but out-of-core.
HookEvent Payload passed from adapter to engine: { sink: SinkType, args: SinkArgs, context_id: ContextId, phase: Entry|Exit, timestamp }.
RequestContext Per-request store: { id, http: HttpInfo, taint_map: TaintMap, call_stack, started } — isolated per thread or async task; never shared across concurrent requests.
Context isolation Invariant: RequestContext is scoped strictly to one logical request; no taint or correlation data leaks across concurrent requests/tasks regardless of concurrency model.
Inline guard The composed pipeline rasp-protect executes inline in every request at every sink: hook → context → taint → detect → policy → action; must complete within overhead budget.
Monitor mode Policy.mode == Monitor — guard observes and emits telemetry for every detection but NEVER alters app behavior; no blocking, no error injection. Monitor purity invariant.
Block mode Policy.mode == Block — guard aborts the sink operation and returns a safe error to the app on Action::Block; the app continues without executing the dangerous operation.
Monitor purity Invariant: in Monitor mode zero side effects on the app's observable behavior — no aborted ops, no injected exceptions, no altered return values.
Block determinism Invariant: given identical (sink args, RequestContext, Policy), the Allow/Block decision and attached evidence are identical — no stochastic or time-dependent branching.
Fail-open Policy.fail_mode == Open — on RASP internal error, allow the operation to proceed and log the fault; prioritizes availability over security.
Fail-closed Policy.fail_mode == Closed — on RASP internal error, block the operation and log the fault; prioritizes security over availability.
Host-safety invariant A RASP fault NEVER crashes or hangs the protected app — every hook path is bounded; panics are caught at the hook boundary; errors fall back to fail_mode, never propagate into host.
Overhead budget Inline added latency constraint — p95 added latency ≤ configured threshold (default 5%); analyzers short-circuit on untainted args; taint propagation is cost-bounded.
Detection Result of a successful attack analysis: { rule_id, sink, severity, confidence, evidence, taint_path } — produced by rasp-detect, consumed by rasp-policy.
Action Policy enforcement outcome: Allow | Block | Monitor; Block triggers sink abort; Monitor allows but emits event; Allow is silent pass-through.
Rule Named detection + enforcement spec: { id, sink, kind: Signature|Semantic, action } — keyed by rule_id in Policy.rules.
Policy Top-level config: { mode, rules: Vec<Rule>, waivers: Vec<Waiver>, fail_mode }; hot-reloadable without restart; transitions must not drop in-flight requests.
Virtual patching Rule targeting a known CVE in a dependency; blocks known-bad inputs without modifying the vulnerable library; identified by CVE-id waiver or rule condition.
Waiver Scoped allow-list entry: exempts a (rule_id, context_matcher) pair from blocking; must carry expiry, owner, and justification fields.
SQLi (SQL injection) Attack class: untrusted input inserted into a SQL query alters its structure/semantics; detected by tokenizing the query and identifying injected structural tokens not present in the template.
Command injection Attack class: untrusted input inserted into an OS command exec alters the command; detected by argument-level parsing to find shell metacharacters or injected tokens.
Path traversal Attack class: untrusted input in a file path contains ../ sequences or absolute paths that escape the intended root; detected by normalizing the resolved path against an allowlist.
SSRF Server-Side Request Forgery — attacker-controlled URL in an outbound HTTP call targets internal infrastructure; detected via URL allowlist + private-IP/loopback check on resolved host.
Deserialization gadget A class chain in a runtime's class hierarchy that produces harmful side effects when an attacker-controlled serialized blob is deserialized; detected by class-name and magic-byte signature matching before full deserialization.
XXE XML eXternal Entity injection — malicious XML declares external entities; parser is redirected to local files or internal services; detected by scanning the raw XML prolog for ENTITY / SYSTEM / PUBLIC declarations.
LDAP injection Untrusted input inserted into an LDAP filter alters its structure; detected by filter-syntax tokenization looking for injected operators or wildcard abuse.
NoSQL injection Untrusted input inserted into a NoSQL query object (JSON/BSON) alters query semantics; detected by type checks (unexpected $operator keys) and structural analysis.
Signature rule Detection rule matching fixed patterns — byte sequences, token sets, regex; fast but bypassed by novel encodings; complemented by semantic rules.
Semantic rule Detection rule performing structural/syntactic analysis of the sink argument (tokenize SQL, parse URL, normalize path) — higher precision than signatures, more CPU cost.
False positive (FP) RASP blocks or flags a benign operation; degrades availability and erodes operator trust; minimized by taint-gating (only analyze tainted args) and semantic precision.
False negative (FN) RASP misses a real attack; leads to exploitation; managed by rule coverage, taint soundness, and periodic red-team corpus regression.
SIEM/CEF Security Information and Event Management / Common Event Format — standardized attack event schema emitted by rasp-telemetry to security monitoring infrastructure.
IAST Interactive Application Security Testing — contrast to RASP: IAST instruments for vulnerability discovery during testing, not production protection; RASP is a production enforcement layer.
WAF Web Application Firewall (project 338) — network-edge defense; sees only HTTP bytes, lacks execution context, higher FP rate than RASP for injection; complementary layer.
OpenRASP Baidu open-source RASP reference implementation (Java); hook model and policy semantics are a primary reference for this project's architecture.
ContextId Opaque identifier correlating a HookEvent to its RequestContext; generated at request ingress; propagated via thread-local or async task-local storage.
SinkArgs Tagged union of arguments passed to a sink, typed per SinkType; carries the raw data subjected to taint check and attack analysis.
TaintMap Per-request map from value identity to Vec<TaintTag>; bounded capacity; evicts on overflow with conservative full-taint fallback.
Cost budget (taint) Per-request operation counter for taint propagation; when exceeded, remaining values are conservatively marked fully tainted and a metric is emitted.
Attack event Structured record emitted on detection/block: includes rule_id, sink, severity, confidence, evidence, taint_path, request_id, timestamp; exported via CEF/JSON.
Severity Critical | High | Medium | Low — assigned per rule; used by rasp-policy to prioritize blocking and by rasp-telemetry for alert routing.

Appendix A — File Inventory

rasp-core/
  Cargo.toml                        — crate manifest, no dep on other rasp-* crates
  src/lib.rs                        — re-exports all public types; feature flags
  src/types/sink.rs                 — SinkType enum + SinkArgs tagged union
  src/types/event.rs                — HookEvent struct, Phase enum (Entry|Exit)
  src/types/context.rs              — RequestContext, HttpInfo, CallStack, ContextId
  src/types/taint.rs                — TaintSource, TaintTag, TaintedValue, TaintMap
  src/types/detection.rs            — Detection, Severity, Confidence, Evidence, TaintPath
  src/types/policy.rs               — Action, Rule, RuleKind, Policy, Waiver, FailMode
  src/types/error.rs                — RaspError enum, thiserror impl
  src/serde.rs                      — serde impls for all public types (JSON + CEF)
  src/ids.rs                        — ContextId, RuleId, WaiverId newtype wrappers + generation
  src/limits.rs                     — compile-time + config constants (budget defaults, max stack depth)
  tests/types_roundtrip.rs          — serde roundtrip property tests for all core types

rasp-hook/
  Cargo.toml                        — depends: rasp-core
  src/lib.rs                        — re-exports hook API
  src/abi.rs                        — extern "C" hook ABI symbols (rasp_hook_entry, rasp_hook_exit)
  src/registry.rs                   — SinkRegistry: map SinkType -> Vec<HookFn>, register/deregister
  src/dispatch.rs                   — multi-sink dispatch loop; invokes all registered hooks per sink
  src/adapter.rs                    — RuntimeAdapter trait; contract for per-runtime shims
  src/trampoline.rs                 — trampoline helpers: save/restore calling convention state
  src/safety.rs                     — catch-unwind boundary + panic-to-error converter at ABI edge
  src/ffi.rs                        — C-compatible struct layouts for cross-language adapter use
  benches/dispatch_bench.rs         — dispatch overhead micro-benchmark (criterion)
  tests/abi_roundtrip.rs            — ABI entry/exit pairing tests
  tests/registry_test.rs            — register/deregister/dispatch correctness tests

rasp-context/
  Cargo.toml                        — depends: rasp-core
  src/lib.rs                        — re-exports context API
  src/store.rs                      — per-request store: thread_local! + tokio::task_local! variants
  src/request.rs                    — HttpInfo capture: method, path, headers, body ref, query params
  src/stack.rs                      — CallStack: push/pop frames, depth limit, async-safe
  src/lifecycle.rs                  — request begin/end hooks; context creation and teardown
  src/propagation.rs                — async context propagation helpers (spawn wrappers, future adapters)
  src/correlation.rs                — ContextId generation (UUIDv7), request-id header extraction
  tests/isolation_test.rs           — concurrent-request context isolation property tests
  tests/async_propagation_test.rs   — tokio task-local propagation correctness tests
  tests/lifecycle_test.rs           — begin/end symmetry, double-end detection tests

rasp-taint/
  Cargo.toml                        — depends: rasp-core, rasp-context
  src/lib.rs                        — re-exports taint API
  src/source.rs                     — HTTP source tagging: extract TaintedValues from HttpInfo
  src/propagation.rs                — string operation rules: concat, slice, format, to_lowercase, etc.
  src/cost.rs                       — per-request operation counter; budget enforcement; overflow policy
  src/sink_check.rs                 — is_tainted(value, taint_map) -> Option<Vec<TaintTag>>
  src/map.rs                        — TaintMap impl: bounded capacity, LRU eviction, conservative fallback
  src/metadata.rs                   — TaintTag construction, byte_range arithmetic, merge/intersect
  tests/propagation_test.rs         — propagation rules unit tests: concat, slice, split, format
  tests/soundness_test.rs           — no-drop invariant tests within budget; over-budget conservative behavior
  tests/cost_test.rs                — budget enforcement, counter reset per request, overflow handling
  tests/isolation_test.rs           — TaintMap does not leak across simulated concurrent requests

rasp-detect/
  Cargo.toml                        — depends: rasp-core, rasp-taint
  src/lib.rs                        — re-exports detect API; Analyzer trait
  src/analyzer.rs                   — Analyzer trait: fn analyze(event, taint) -> Option<Detection>
  src/sqli.rs                       — SQL injection: tokenize query, detect injected structural tokens
  src/cmd.rs                        — Command injection: arg parsing, shell metachar detection

  src/deser.rs                      — Deserialization: class-name list + magic-byte signature matching
  src/xxe.rs                        — XXE: XML prolog scan for ENTITY/SYSTEM/PUBLIC declarations
  src/ldap.rs                       — LDAP injection: filter tokenizer, operator injection detection
  src/nosql.rs                      — NoSQL injection: JSON object key scan for $operator injection
  src/rules.rs                      — Rule loader: parse YAML/JSON rules, map to Analyzer dispatch
  src/scoring.rs                    — confidence scoring: taint strength + rule match depth -> Confidence
  src/short_circuit.rs              — fast-path: skip analyzer if no taint on sink args
  benches/analyzer_bench.rs         — per-analyzer latency benchmark with attack + benign corpora
  tests/sqli_test.rs                — SQLi attack/benign corpus regression tests
  tests/cmd_test.rs                 — command injection corpus regression tests

  tests/short_circuit_test.rs       — untainted args produce no Detection, with latency assertion

0 Workspace scaffold; rasp-core types; all crate skeletons; deny/clippy/rustfmt CI green 1 week Type API churn cascades across crates; serde schema instability All core types compile; serde roundtrip tests pass; CI green

4 rasp-detect: all 8 analyzers, Analyzer trait, short-circuit, confidence scoring 3 weeks False-positive rate on benign corpus; libinjection tokenizer edge cases; XXE parser variance All attack corpora detected; benign corpus zero FP; per-analyzer latency within budget
5 rasp-policy: engine, loader, hot-reload, waivers, virtual patching, fail-mode 1 week Hot-reload races with in-flight requests; waiver expiry clock drift Reload under concurrent load: no dropped/mis-applied decisions; waiver expiry tests pass
6 rasp-protect: InlineGuard pipeline, block/monitor paths, overhead tracking 1 week Pipeline stage error not contained; block path leaks partial state to app End-to-end pipeline tests pass; Monitor purity tests green; host-safety induced-panic test pass

10 Integration + hardening: end-to-end with mock instrumented app, chaos tests, policy reload under load 2 weeks Mock adapter fidelity gap; chaos-induced panic escaping host-safety boundary Full pipeline integration tests pass; chaos suite: no app crash, no hang; overhead budget holds
11 Observability + operational: runbook, alert rules, Grafana dashboard, SIEM integration test 1 week Alert fatigue from noisy metrics; dashboard staleness Runbook reviewed; all alert expressions tested against synthetic event streams

13 Release hardening: MSRV check, deny audit, license scan, SBOM generation, changelog, tag 1 week Transitive dep advisory; license incompatibility in new deps cargo deny check clean; SBOM generated; MSRV 1.78 verified; release tag created
